Lemma 2.7.22. With assumptions and notation as in Proposition 2.7.21, if (πi) is a family of
pairwise non-isomorphic irreducible representations of G, the isotypic subspaces M(πi) ⊂E are in
direct sum.

Proof. Indeed, for any fixed π in this family, the intersection of M(π) with the sum of all
M(πi), π1 ̸= π, is necessarily zero: it cannot contain any irreducible subrepresentation, since the
possibilities coming from π are incompatible with those coming from the other πi.
□

Exercise 2.7.23 (Isotypic components when k is not algebraically closed). Let A be a ring
with unit, which may not be commutative. Given a right A-module M (which means that ma is
defined for m ∈M and a ∈A, with the expected rules, such that (m1 + m2)a = m1a + m2a), a left
A-module N, and an abelian group V, a Z-linear map b : M × N −→V is said to be A-bilinear if
we have
b(ma, n) = b(m, an)
for all m ∈M, n ∈N and a ∈A.
Then one can show (imitating the usual construction of
the tensor product over commutative rings) that there exists an abelian group M ⊗A N, called
the tensor product of M and N over A, and an A-bilinear map ⊗: M × N −→M ⊗A N which
“represents” all A-bilinear maps, in the sense that for any abelian group V and for any A-bilinear

map b : M × N −→V, there exists a unique homomorphism M ⊗A N
˜b
−→V of abelian groups such
that
b(m, n) = ˜b(m ⊗n)
for all m in M and n in N.
Let G be a group, let k be a field and let ϱ : G −→GL(E) be a finite-dimensional k-
representation of G and π : G −→GL(F) a finite-dimensional irreducible k-representation of
G.
(1) Let A = EndG(π). Show that HomG(F, E) is naturally a right A-module and that F is
naturally a left A-module.
(2) Let ME(π) be the π-isotypic component of ϱ. Show that there exists a natural isomorphism
of abelian groups
ME(π) ≃HomG(F, E) ⊗A F.
(3) Show that HomG(F, E) ⊗A F has a natural structure of k-vector space such that the iso-
morphism in (3) is k-linear.
(4) Show that there is a k-linear action of G on HomG(F, E) ⊗A F determined by

g · Φ ⊗v = Φ ⊗ϱ(g)v,

and that the isomorphism in (2) and (3) is then an intertwiner.
If k is algebraically closed, the algebra A is isomorphic to k, and hence is commutative, and
these results are then the same as those of Proposition 2.7.21.

2.7.3. Burnside’s irreducibility criterion and its generalizations, 1. We now show how
to use Schur’s Lemma to prove a result of Burnside which provides a frequently useful irreducibility
criterion for finite-dimensional representations, and we derive further consequences along the same
lines. In fact, we will prove this twice (and a third time in Chapter 4 in the case of finite groups);
in this section, we argue in the style of Burnside, and below in Section 2.7.4, we will recover the
same results in the style of Frobenius.
We will motivate the first result, Burnside’s criterion, from the following point of view: given
a finite-dimensional k-representation ϱ of a group G, acting on the vector space E, the image of ϱ
is a subset of the vector space Endk(E). We ask then “what are the linear relations satisfied by
these elements?” For instance, the block-diagonal shape (2.33) of a representation which is is not

71


irreducible shows clearly some relations: those that express that the matrices in the bases indicated
have lower-left corner(s) equal to 0, for instance. These are obvious. Are there others?

Theorem 2.7.24 (Burnside’s irreducibility criterion). Let k be an algebraically closed field, G
a group. A finite-dimensional k-representation

ϱ : G −→GL(E)

is irreducible if and only if the image of ϱ satisfies no non-trivial linear relation in Endk(E).
Equivalently, ϱ is irreducible if and only if the linear span of the image of ϱ in Endk(E) is equal to
Endk(E).

The proof we give is a modern version of Burnside’s original argument. One can give much
shorter proofs – the one in the next section is an example – but this one has the advantage of
“exercising” the basic formalism of representation theory, and of being easy to motivate.

Proof. First of all, the two statements we give are equivalent by duality of finite-dimensional
k-vector spaces. More precisely, let V = Endk(E); then by “relations satisfied by the image of ϱ”,
we mean the k-linear subspace

R = {ϕ ∈V′ | ⟨ϕ, ϱ(g)⟩= 0 for all g ∈G},

of V′, the linear dual of Endk(E). Then we are saying that R = 0 if and only if the image of G
spans V, which is part of duality theory.
The strategy of the proof is going to be the following:

(1) For some natural representation of G on V′, we show that R is a subrepresentation;
(2) We find an explicit decomposition of V′ (with its G-action) as a direct sum of irreducible
representations, embedded in V′ in a specific manner;
(3) Using this description, we can see what the possibilities for R are, and especially that
R = 0 if ϱ is irreducible.
This strategy will also be used afterward to give a more general result of comparison of distinct
irreducible representations.
We let G act on V′ by the contragredient of the representation of G on V given by

g · T = ϱ(g) ◦T

for g ∈G and T : E →E. Note that this corresponds to the action (2.20), and not the action (2.18).
To check that R ⊂V′ is a subrepresentation, we need simply note that if ϕ ∈R and g ∈G, then
we have
⟨g · ϕ, ϱ(h)⟩= ⟨ϕ, g−1 · ϱ(h)⟩= ⟨ϕ, ϱ(g−1h)⟩= 0

for all h ∈G, which means that g · ϕ is also in R.
From (2.21), we know that V – with the above action – is isomorphic to a direct sum of
dim ϱ copies of ϱ; hence V′ is isomorphic to a direct sum of the same number of copies of the
contragredient qϱ of ϱ, which we know to be irreducible (Proposition 2.2.19). It follows that any
irreducible subrepresentation π of R (which exists if R ̸= 0) must be itself isomorphic to qϱ. (This
fact is clear from the Jordan-H¨older-Noether Theorem, but can be seen directly also by considering
the composites
pi : π �→R �→V′ ≃
�

i⩽dim ϱ
Wi −→Wi ≃qϱ,

where Wi are subrepresentations of V′ isomorphic to qϱ; each of these composites is in HomG(π, qϱ),
and hence is either 0 or an isomorphism, by Schur’s Lemma; since π ̸= 0, not all pi can be zero,
hence π ≃qϱ.)

72


However, we claim that there is an isomorphism (of k-vector spaces)
�

HomG(E′, V′)
v

αv

where αv : E′ →V′ is defined by
⟨αv(λ), T⟩= ⟨λ, T(v)⟩
for λ ∈E′ and T : E →E. If this is the case, then assuming that R ̸= 0, and hence that R contains
a copy of qϱ, means that, for some v ̸= 0, the image of αv is in R. But this implies that for all
λ ∈E′, and g ∈G, we have
0 = ⟨αv(λ), ϱ(g)⟩= ⟨λ, ϱ(g)v⟩
which is impossible even for a single g, since ϱ(g)v ̸= 0.
Checking the claim is not very difficult; we leave it to the reader to verify that each αv is indeed
a G-homomorphism from E′ to V′, and that v �→αv is k-linear. We then observe that

dim HomG(E′, V′) = dim HomG(qϱ, (dim E)qϱ) = dim(E) dimG(qϱ, qϱ) = dim E

by Schur’s Lemma again (using the fact that k is algebraically closed). So the map will be an
isomorphism as soon as it is injective. However, αv = 0 means that

⟨λ, T(v)⟩= 0

for all λ ∈E′ and T ∈V, and that only happens when v = 0 (take T to be the identity).
□

Example 2.7.25. Consider again the representation ϱ of R/Z by rotations in the plane of

In particular, it does satisfy non-trivial relations like “the diagonal coefficients are
equal”.

We emphasize again the strategy we used, because it is a common pattern in applications of
representation theory: one wishes to analyze a certain vector space (here, the relation space R);
this space is seen to be a subspace of a bigger one, on which a group G acts, and then the space is
seen to be a subrepresentation of this bigger space; independent analysis of the latter is performed,
e.g., a decomposition in irreducible summands; and then one deduces a priori restrictions on the
possibilities for the space of original interest. (For another concrete application, see Section 4.7.3.)
We now implement this principle again in a generalization (due to Frobenius and Schur) of
Burnside’s Theorem. To motivate it, consider a finite-dimensional k-representation of G

ϱ : G −→GL(E)

which is irreducible, with k algebraically closed. Burnside’s Theorem means, in particular, that if
we fix a basis (vi) of E and express ϱ in the basis (vi), producing the homomorphism

ϱm : G −→GLdim(E)(k)

the resulting “matrix coefficients” (ϱm

The interest of these matrix coefficients is that they are functions on G (with values in k); as
such, they might be written down without mentioning the representation at all, and in particular
without knowing the representation space. However, the choice of basis is annoying, so the following
definition is more convenient:

Definition 2.7.26 (Matrix coefficient). Let G be a group, k a field and

ϱ : G −→GL(E)

a k-representation of G. A matrix coefficient of ϱ is any function on G of the type

fv,λ

�

for some fixed v ∈E and λ ∈E′.

Remark 2.7.27. Note that these functions are typically not multiplicative. An exception is
when ϱ : G −→GL1(k) = k× is a one-dimensional representation; in that case one can take
v = 1 ∈k and λ the identity of k, so that the matrix coefficient is equal to ϱ as a function G −→k.

Now we come back to the discussion: matrix coefficients of a fixed irreducible representation
are k-linearly independent. What is more natural than to ask: “What about different representa-
tions?” Is it possible that their matrix coefficients satisfy non-trivial linear relations? The answer
is very satisfactory: No! This is another expression of the fact that distinct (i.e., non-isomorphic)
irreducible representations of a group are “independent”.

Example 2.7.29 (Linear independence of one-dimensional representations). Following on Re-
mark 2.7.27, since any one-dimensional representation is irreducible, and two of them are isomor-
phic if and only if they coincide as functions G −→k×, the theorem shows that any family of
homomorphisms
χi : G −→k× ⊂k,
is k-linearly independent in Ck(G) when k is algebraically closed. But in fact, this last assumption
is not needed, because we can see the representations χi as taking values in an arbitrary alge-
braic closure of k, and they remain irreducible when seen in this manner, like all 1-dimensional
representations.
This result is important in Galois theory. As one might expect, it is possible to prove it more
directly and elementarily, and the reader should attempt to do it (see, e.g., [44, Th. VI.4.1]).

The linear independence of matrix coefficients turns out to have many important applications.
In particular, it gives quite precise information on the structure of the regular representation of G
acting on the space Ck(G) of k-valued functions on the group.

Corollary 2.7.30 (Matrix coefficients as subrepresentations of the regular representation). Let
G be a group, k an algebraically closed field, and ϱ a finite-dimensional irreducible k-representation
of G. Let M(ϱ) be the subspace of Ck(G) spanned by all matrix coefficients fv,λ of ϱ.
(1) The space M(ϱ) depends only on ϱ up to isomorphism.

19 This is the crucial point, where the “independence” of distinct irreducible representations comes into play.

76


(2) It is a subrepresentation of the regular representation of G acting on Ck(G); moreover M(ϱ)
is semisimple and isomorphic to a direct sum of dim(ϱ) copies of ϱ.
(3) Any subrepresentation of Ck(G) isomorphic to ϱ is contained in the subspace M(ϱ), i.e.,
M(ϱ) is the ϱ-isotypic component of Ck(G), as defined in Proposition 2.7.9.

For one-dimensional representations ϱ (e.g., ϱ = 1), we already computed M(ϱ) in Exam-
ple 2.1.9: it is a one-dimensional space, spanned by ϱ seen as a k-valued function. This verifies (3)
directly in these simple cases.

Proof. We first check (1), which states that M(ϱ) is a canonical subspace of Ck(G). Let E be
the space on which ϱ acts and let τ : G −→GL(F) be a k-representation isomorphic to ϱ, with
the linear map
Φ : E −→F

giving this isomorphism. Then for any w ∈F and λ ∈F′, writing w = Φ(v) for some v ∈E, we
have

fw,λ(g) = ⟨λ, τ(g)w⟩F
= ⟨λ, τ(g)Φ(v)⟩F
= ⟨λ, Φ(ϱ(g)v)⟩F
= ⟨tΦ(λ), ϱ(g)v⟩E = fv,tΦ(λ)(g),

for all g ∈G, showing that any matrix coefficient for τ is also one for ϱ. By symmetry, we see that
M(ϱ) and M(τ) are equal subspaces of Ck(G).
We next check that M(ϱ) ⊂Ck(G) is indeed a subrepresentation: for v ∈E (the space on which
G acts), λ ∈E′, and g ∈G, we have

ϱG(g)fv,λ(x) = fv,λ(xg) = ⟨λ, ϱ(xg)v⟩= fϱ(g)v,λ(x) ∈M(ϱ).

In fact, this formula says more: it shows that, for a fixed λ ∈E′, the linear map

Φλ : v �→fv,λ

is an intertwining operator between ϱ and M(ϱ).
Fix a basis (vj) of the space E on which ϱ acts, and let (λj) be the dual basis. By construction,
M(ϱ) is spanned by the matrix-coefficients

fi,j = fvi,λj,
1 ⩽i, j ⩽dim(ϱ)

and from the linear independence of matrix coefficients, these functions form a basis of the space
M(ϱ). In particular, we have dim M(ϱ) = dim(ϱ)2. Now the intertwining operator

Φ =
�

i
Φλi :
�

is surjective (since its image contains each basis vector fi,j), and both sides have the same dimen-
sion. Hence it must be an isomorphism, which shows that M(ϱ) is isomorphic to (dim(ϱ))ϱ as a
representation of G.
There only remains to check the last part. Let E ⊂Ck(G) be a subrepresentation of the regular
representation that is isomorphic to ϱ. To show that E ⊂M(ϱ), we will check that the elements
f ∈E, which are functions on G, are all matrix coefficients of E. Let δ ∈Ck(G)′ be the linear form
defined by
δ(f) = f(1)

77


for f ∈Ck(G). Consider then the linear form δE ∈E′ that is the restriction of δ to E. Then, for
any function f ∈E and x ∈G, we have

⟨δE, ϱG(x)f⟩= ϱG(x)f(1) = f(x).

(by definition of the regular representation.)
The left-hand side (seen as a function of x) is a matrix coefficient for ϱ, since ϱG on E is
isomorphic to ϱ, and hence we see that f ∈M(ϱ).
□

The next corollary will be improved in the chapter on representations of finite groups. We state
it here because it is the first a priori restriction we have found on irreducible representations for
certain groups:

Corollary 2.7.31. Let G be a finite group and let k be an algebraically closed field. There are
only finitely many irreducible k-representations of G up to isomorphism, and they satisfy
�

ϱ
(dim ϱ)2 ⩽|G|

where the sum is over isomorphism classes of irreducible k-representations of G.

Proof. First of all, since the space of an irreducible representation of a finite group is spanned
by finitely many vectors ϱ(g)v, g ∈G (for any vector v ̸= 0), any irreducible representation of G is
finite-dimensional.
Then by the previous corollary, for any such irreducible representation ϱ, the regular represen-
tation ϱG contains a subspace isomorphic to dim(ϱ) copies of ϱ. By the linear independence of
matrix coefficients of non-isomorphic irreducible representations (Theorem 2.7.28), the sum over ϱ
of these representations is a direct sum and has dimension
�

ϱ
(dim ϱ)2,

hence the result since the dimension of the regular representation is |G|.
□

Remark 2.7.32. If G is finite, there is equality in this formula if and only if the regular

In the next section, we will derive further consequences of the linear independence of matrix
coefficients, related to characters of finite-dimensional representations. Before this, as another appli-
cation of Schur’s Lemma and its corollaries, we can now prove Proposition 2.3.23 about irreducible
representations of a direct product G = G1 × G2.

78


Proof of Proposition 2.3.23. Recall that we are considering an algebraically closed field k
and two groups G1 and G2 and want to prove that all finite-dimensional irreducible k-representa-
tions of G = G1 × G2 are external tensor products of the form ϱ ≃ϱ1 ⊠ϱ2 for some irreducible
representations ϱi of Gi (unique up to isomorphism).
To begin with, if ϱ1 and ϱ2 are finite-dimensional irreducible representations of G1 and G2, the
irreducibility of ϱ = ϱ1 ⊠ϱ2 follows from Burnside’s irreducibility criterion: since the ϱ1(g1) and
ϱ2(g2), for gi ∈Gi, span the k-linear endomorphism spaces of their respective spaces, it follows by
elementary linear algebra that the ϱ1(g1) ⊗ϱ2(g2) also span the endomorphism space of the tensor
product. (Here we used the fact that k is algebraically closed.)
Thus what matters is to prove the converse. Therefore, let

ϱ : G1 × G2 −→GL(E)

be an irreducible k-representation. We restrict ϱ to the subgroup G1 = G1 × {1} ⊂G, and we let
E1 ⊂E be an irreducible subrepresentation of E seen as representation of G1; we denote by ϱ1 the
corresponding “abstract” representation of G1. We now proceed to find a representation ϱ2 of G2
such that
ϱ1 ⊠ϱ2 ≃ϱ.

For this purpose, define the k-vector space

E2 = HomG1(ϱ1, ResG
G1 ϱ),

of intertwiners between ϱ1 and the restriction of ϱ to G1; note that it is non-zero by definition of
ϱ1. We claim that the definition

(ϱ2(g2)Φ)(v) = ϱ(1, g2)Φ(v)

for g2 ∈G2, Φ ∈E2 and v ∈E1, defines a representation ϱ2 of G2 = {1} × G2 ⊂G on E2. The
point is that because G1 and G2, seen as subgroups of G, commute with each other, the k-linear
map ϱ2(g2)Φ is still a homomorphism ϱ1 →ϱ (and not merely a linear map). Indeed, denoting
Ψ = ϱ2(g2)Φ, we compute

Ψ(ϱ1(g1)v) = ϱ(1, g2)Φ(ϱ1(g1)v)

= ϱ(1, g2)(ϱ(g1, 1)Φ(v))
(since Φ ∈E2)

= ϱ(g1, 1)(ϱ(1, g2)Φ(v)) = ϱ(g1, 1)Ψ(v),

for all v ∈E1, which is to say, Ψ ∈E2.
Now we define a k-linear map

Θ
�
E1 ⊗E2
−→
E
v ⊗Φ
�→
Φ(v),

and we claim that Θ is an intertwiner between ϱ1 ⊠ϱ2 and ϱ. Indeed, we can check this on pure
tensors: for gi ∈Gi, v ⊗Φ ∈E1 ⊗E2, we have

Θ(ϱ1 ⊠ϱ2(g1, g2)(v ⊗Φ)) = Θ(ϱ1(g1)v ⊗ϱ2(g2)Φ)

= (ϱ2(g2)Φ)(ϱ1(g1)v)

= ϱ(1, g2)Φ(ϱ1(g1)v)

= ϱ(1, g2)ϱ(g1, 1)Φ(v)

= ϱ(g1, g2)Φ(v) = ϱ(g1, g2)Θ(v ⊗Φ).

Note that this must really be written down by hand to not look like gibberish.

We will now show that Θ is bijective. Let F2 ⊂E2 be any irreducible subrepresentation (of
G2), with action denoted ˜ϱ2; restricting Θ to E1 ⊗F2 gives an intertwiner

ϱ1 ⊠˜ϱ2 −→E.

By the first part of Proposition 2.3.23, which was proved at the beginning, the representation
ϱ1 ⊠˜ϱ2 of G is irreducible, and so is E by assumption; moreover, if v0 ̸= 0 is a vector in E1 and
0 ̸= Φ0 ∈F2, then Θ(v0 ⊗Φ0) = Φ0(v0) is non-zero (Φ0 is injective, by Schur’s Lemma, because it
is an embedding of the irreducible representation ϱ1 in ϱ). Thus Θ restricted to ϱ1 ⊠˜ϱ2 is non-zero,
and again by Schur’s Lemma, it must be an isomorphism.
We are thus already done proving that ϱ is an external tensor product, but we will continue
with some (minor) additional work that shows that, in fact, Θ itself is bijective. For this, we just
need to show that
dim(E1 ⊗E2) = dim E = dim(E1) dim(F2),

(since we now know that Θ is surjective) or equivalently that dim F2 = dim E2. But

E2 = HomG1(ϱ1, ResG
G1 ϱ),

and, by fixing a basis (vj) of the space F2 of ˜ϱ2, we see that the restriction to G1 of ϱ = ϱ1 ⊠˜ϱ2 is
the direct sum
�

j
E1 ⊗kvj ≃(dim ˜ϱ2)E1,

so that the dimension of E2 is given by

dim E2 = (dim ˜ϱ2) dim HomG1(ϱ1, ϱ1) = dim ˜ϱ2

by the last part of Schur’s Lemma (we use again the fact that k is algebraically closed).
Finally, coming back to the general situation, note that this last observation on the restriction
of ϱ1⊠ϱ2 to G1 (and the analogue for G2) show that ϱ1 and ϱ2 are indeed unique up to isomorphism,
by the Jordan-H¨older-Noether Theorem.
□

Example 2.7.33. As we have mentioned before, Proposition 2.3.23 is not valid in general for
k-representations of a group G1×G2, if k is not algebraically closed. We give here a simple example.
Let k = R, G1 = G2 = R/Z and G = G1 × G2, and consider the composite

π : G
a
−→R/Z
ϱ
−→GL2(R)

where a(θ1, θ2) = θ1 + θ2 and ϱ is the R-irreducible representation of Example 2.4.1. Since R/Z is
abelian, π is indeed a representation of G over R. It is irreducible, because the addition map a is
surjective, and ϱ is R-irreducible, as we saw. But we claim that it is not isomorphic to an external
tensor product of representations of G1 and G2.
One way to see this, anticipating a bit some elementary parts of Section 2.7.5 below, is to note
that an exterior tensor product τ = ϱ1 ⊠ϱ2 of two finite-dimensional representations ϱ1 and ϱ2
satisfies
Tr(τ(g1, g2)) = Tr(ϱ1(g)) Tr(ϱ2(g))

for all (g1, g2) ∈G1 × G2. Since the traces of isomorphic representations are equal functions on G
(an easy property, see Proposition 2.7.38 for a formal proof), the claim will be established if we
can show that the trace of π is not a product of a function of g1 and a function of g2. But for
(θ1, θ2) ∈G1 × G2, we have
Tr(π(θ1, θ2)) = 2 cos(θ1 + θ2),

and we leave it as an exercise to check that this is not a product function.

2.7.4. Burnside’s theorem and its generalizations, 2. As promised, we now explain how
to recover the results of the previous section in a style closer (maybe) to that of Frobenius. Even
for readers who have fully understood the arguments already used, this may be useful. In fact, the
proofs are simpler, but not so well motivated. The viewpoint is to start this time by determining
directly the isotypic components of the regular representation.

Proposition 2.7.34 (Isotypic component of the regular representation). Let k be an alge-
braically closed field and G a group. For any finite-dimensional irreducible k-representation

ϱ : G −→GL(E)

of G, the ϱ-isotypic component M(ϱ) ⊂Ck(G) of the regular representation is isomorphic to a direct
sum of dim(ϱ) copies of ϱ, and is spanned by the matrix coefficients of ϱ.

Proof. We start with the realization (2.23) of Ck(G) as the induced representation IndG
1 (1),
and then apply Frobenius reciprocity (2.24), which gives us linear isomorphisms

HomG(ϱ, Ck(G)) = HomG(ϱ, IndG
1 (1))

≃Hom1(ResG
1 (ϱ), 1) = Homk(E, k) = E′.

Thus the isotypic component M(ϱ), which is equal to the image of the injective G-homomorphism
�
HomG(ϱ, Ck(G)) ⊗E
−→
Ck(G)
Φ ⊗v
�→
Φ(v)

(see Proposition 2.7.21) is isomorphic to E′ ⊗E as a representation of G, where E′ has the trivial
action of G, and E the action under ϱ. This is the same as the direct sum of dim(E) copies of ϱ.
We now show, also directly, that the image of the linear map above is spanned by matrix
coefficients. Indeed, given λ ∈E′, the corresponding homomorphism

Φλ : E −→Ck(G)

in HomG(ϱ, Ck(G)) is given, according to the recipe in (2.28), by

Φλ(v) = (x �→λ(ϱ(x)v)) = fv,λ,

i.e., its values are indeed matrix coefficients.
□

Re-proof of Theorem 2.7.28. First of all, we recover Burnside’s irreducibility criterion.
Consider an irreducible finite-dimensional representation ϱ : G −→GL(E). We know from the
proposition that
dim M(ϱ) = dim(ϱ)2.
Since M(ϱ) is spanned by the dim(ϱ)2 matrix coefficients

fvi,λj,
1 ⩽i, j ⩽dim ϱ

associated with any basis (vi) of E and the dual basis (λj) of E′, these must be independent. But
then if we consider the matrix representation ϱm of ϱ in the basis (vi), it follows that there is no
non-trivial linear relation between the coefficients of the ϱm(g), and hence – by duality – the span
of those matrices must be the space of all matrices of size dim ϱ, which means that the linear span
of the ϱ(g) in End(E) is equal to End(E). This recovers Burnside’s criterion (since the converse
direction was immediate).
Now consider finitely many irreducible representations (ϱi) which are pairwise non-isomorphic.
The subspaces M(ϱi) ⊂Ck(G) are in direct sum (Lemma 2.7.22: the intersection of any one with
the sum of the others is a subrepresentation where no composition factor is permitted, hence it is
zero), and this means that the matrix coefficients of the ϱi must be linearly independent – in the
sense of the statement of Theorem 2.7.28.
□

81


Exercise 2.7.35. Let G be a group, k an algebraically closed field. Consider the representation
of G × G on Ck(G) by

π(g, h)f(x) = f(g−1xh)

of Exercise 2.1.3. Let ϱ : G →GL(E) be a finite-dimensional irreducible k-representation, and let
M(ϱ) denote as usual the span of the matrix coefficients of ϱ. Show that M(ϱ) is an irreducible
subrepresentation of π, and describe its isomorphism class as representation of G×G as an external
tensor product.

2.7.5. Characters of finite-dimensional representations. As another consequence of The-
orem 2.7.28, we see that if we are given one matrix coefficient of each of ϱ1 and ϱ2, some irreducible
k-representations of G, both finite-dimensional, we are certain that they will be distinct functions
if ϱ1 and ϱ2 are not isomorphic. The converse is not true, since even a single representation has
typically many matrix coefficients. However, one can combine some of them in such a way that
one obtains a function which only depends on the representation up to isomorphism, and which
characterizes (finite-dimensional) irreducible representations, up to isomorphism.

Definition 2.7.36 (Characters). Let G be a group and k a field.
(1) A character of G over k, or k-character of G, is any function χ : G −→k of the type

χ(g) = Tr ϱ(g)

where ϱ is a finite-dimensional k-representation of G. One also says that χ is the character of G.
(2) An irreducible character of G over k is a character associated to an irreducible k-representation
of G.

Remark 2.7.37. One can, in certain contexts, define characters even for infinite-dimensional
representations (see, e.g., [40, Ch. X], which discusses the important work of Harish-Chandra in
this direction), and obtain a formalism with properties similar to the finite-dimensional case. This
requires many tools from functional analysis and is much deeper than what we are discussing here.

We will typically write χϱ for the character of a given representation ϱ. Then we have:

Proposition 2.7.38. Let G be a group and let k be a field.
(1) Two isomorphic finite-dimensional representations ϱ1 and ϱ2 of G have the same character
as function on G.
(2) If k is algebraically closed, then two irreducible finite-dimensional representations ϱ1 and
ϱ2 of G are isomorphic if and only if their characters are equal as functions on G. More generally,
the characters of the irreducible finite-dimensional representations of G, up to isomorphism, are
linearly independent in Ck(G).
(3) If k is algebraically closed and of characteristic zero, then any two finite-dimensional
semisimple representations of G are isomorphic if and only if their characters are equal.

Proof. For (1), if two representations (irreducible or not, but assumed to be finite-dimensional)
ϱ1 and ϱ2 are isomorphic, with Φ : E1 →E2 giving this isomorphism, we have

Φ ◦ϱ1(g) ◦Φ−1 = ϱ2(g)

for all g ∈G, and hence, by the invariance of the trace of endomorphisms under conjugation, we
obtain
Tr(ϱ1(g)) = Tr(ϱ2(g))

so that their characters are equal.

82


Now, to prove the converse assertion in (2), we note simply that the character Tr ϱ(g) is a sum
of (diagonal) matrix coefficients: if (vi) is a basis of the space of ϱ, with dual basis (λi), we have

χϱ =
�

i
fvi,λi

(i.e,

Tr ϱ(g) =
�

i
⟨λi, ϱ(g)vi⟩,

for all g ∈G). Hence an equality
χϱ1 = χϱ2
is a linear relation between certain matrix coefficients of ϱ1 and ϱ2 respectively. If ϱ1 and ϱ2 are
irreducible but not isomorphic, it follows that such a relation is impossible.
Similarly, expanding in terms of matrix coefficients, we see that any linear relation
�

π
α(π)χπ = 0

among characters of the irreducible finite-dimensional k-representations of G (taken up to isomor-
phism) must have α(π) = 0 for all π.
Finally, for (3), let ϱ be a semisimple k-representation. If we write the decomposition

ϱ ≃
�

nπ(ϱ)π

in terms of the irreducible finite-dimensional k-representations π of G (up to isomorphism), with

nϱ(π)χπ.

By (1), if χϱ1 = χϱ2 for two (semisimple finite-dimensional) representations, we must have
nπ(ϱ1) = nπ(ϱ2) for all π.
But this equality is an equality in k (the characters are k-valued
functions); if k has characteristic zero, this implies the corresponding equality of integers, from
which we see that ϱ1 and ϱ2 are indeed isomorphic.
□

Example 2.7.39 (A zero character). Continuing with the notation of the last proof, if k has
positive characteristic p, it is possible that the integer nπ(ϱ1) −nπ(ϱ2) is a multiple of p for all
π, and then the characters of ϱ1 and ϱ2 are the same. Here is an easy example showing that this
indeed happens. Consider any subgroup G of the group Un(Z/pZ) of unipotent upper-triangular
n × n-matrices with coefficients in Z/pZ, for instance, a subgroup of

Example 2.7.40 (The character of the regular representation). Let k be any field, and let G
be a finite group, so that the space Ck(G) of the regular representation of G is finite-dimensional.
Then its character is given by

(2.47)
χ(g) =

�
|G|
if g = 1
0
if g ̸= 1.

Indeed, we can take as a basis of Ck(G) the family of functions (δx)x∈G equal to 1 at g = x and
0 elsewhere. Then
ϱG(g)δx = δxg−1

for all g and x ∈G. This means that ϱG(g) acts on the basis vectors by permuting them, so that
the corresponding matrix is a permutation matrix. The trace of ϱG(g) is the number of fixed points
of this permutation, but we see that ϱG(g)δx = δx if and only if g = 1, and then x is arbitrary.
This gives the formula we claimed. (Note that here also, if the order of the group G is zero in k,
the character becomes identically zero.)

Example 2.7.41 (Character of the complex conjugate). We can now justify the remark in
Example 2.4.2 concerning the complex conjugate ¯ϱ of a finite-dimensional complex representation
ϱ of a group G. We have
χ¯ϱ(g) = χϱ(g),

since (as explained in the paragraph before that example) the matrix representing ¯ϱ(g) in a fixed
basis is the conjugate of the matrix representing ϱ(g). Since ¯ϱ is semisimple if and only if ϱ is
(again, this was explained before Example 2.4.2) it follows that if ϱ semisimple, we have ϱ ≃¯ϱ if
and only if the character of ϱ is real-valued.

Proposition 2.7.38 is quite remarkable. It gives a tool to study representations of a group using
only functions on G, and – especially for finite and compact groups – it is so successful that in
some cases, one can know all the characters of irreducible representations of a group – as explicit
functions – without knowing explicit descriptions of the corresponding representations!

Exercise 2.7.42 (Characters are unique). The character of a representation can be interpreted
as a linear combination of its matrix coefficients, which turns out to be independent of the specific
choice of model of the representation, in the sense of depending only on its isomorphism class. Can
we construct any other similar function?
(1) Let k be a field. Explain why finding a linear combination of matrix coefficients for k-
representations of dimension n (of any group) which is an invariant under isomorphism amounts
to finding a linear form λ : Mn(k) −→k such that λ(gxg−1) = λ(x) for all g ∈GLn(k) and
x ∈Mn(k).
(2) Prove that the trace is, up to multiplication by a scalar, the only such linear form. [Hint: The
problem is that of finding the GLn(k)-invariants for the contragredient of the natural conjugation
action of GLn(k) on Mn(k).]

Part of the appeal of characters is that they are quite manageable in various computations.
The following summarizes some of their formal properties:

Proposition 2.7.43 (Formalism of characters). Let G be a group and let k be a field. For any
finite-dimensional k-representation ϱ of G, the character χϱ satisfies

(2.48)
χϱ(gxg−1) = χϱ(x),
χϱ(xg) = χϱ(gx)

for all g, x ∈G.

(seen as an element of k),
χϱ1⊕ϱ2 = χϱ1 + χϱ2,
χϱ1⊗ϱ2 = χϱ1χϱ2,

χqϱ(g) = χϱ(g−1).

If ϱ is finite-dimensional and has distinct composition factors ϱi, with multiplicities ni ⩾1,
then

The two statements in (2.48) are equivalent, and state that the value of a character at some
x ∈G only depends on the conjugacy class of x in G. Functions with this property are usually
called class functions on G.
Note that we restrict to a finite-index subgroup for induction because otherwise the dimension
of the induced representation is not finite.
The character formula (2.49) makes sense because the property that sgs−1 be in H, and the
value of the trace of ϱ(sgs−1), are both unchanged if s is replaced by any other element of the coset
Hs. At least if k has characteristic zero, one can also use this remark to rewrite this formula as

since the sum on the right-hand side is equal to |H| times the expression in (2.49).
It may also be useful to observe that one can not use the invariance of χϱ under conjugation
to remove the s in χϱ(sgs−1), since ϱ is only a representation of H, and usually s /∈H.
Note also that by taking g = 1, this formula implies

dim IndG
H(ϱ) = [G : H] dim ϱ

which recovers Proposition 2.3.11) (as it should), if the field k has characteristic zero (in which case
the equality in k gives the same in Z).

Proof. The first formulas are direct consequences of the definitions and the properties of
the trace of linear maps. Similarly, the formula for the restriction is clear, and only the case of
induction requires proof. The argument will be an elaboration of the proof of the dimension formula
(Proposition 2.3.11, or of Proposition 2.3.15.
Let E be the space on which ϱ acts, and let F be the space

F = {f : G →E | f(hx) = ϱ(h)f(x) for h ∈H, x ∈G}

of the induced representation. We will compute the trace by decomposing F (as a linear space)
conveniently, much as was done in the proof of Proposition 2.3.11 when computing the dimension
of F (which is also, of course, the value of the character at g = 1). First of all, for any s ∈G, let

85


Fs ⊂F be the subspace of those f ∈F which vanish for all x /∈Hs; thus Fs only depends on the
coset Hs ∈H\G. We have a direct sum decomposition

F =
�

s∈H\G
Fs,

where the components of a given f are just obtained by taking the restrictions of f to the cosets
Hs and extending this by zero outside Hs.
Now, for a fixed g ∈G, the action of Ind(g) on F is given by the formula

Ind(g)f(x) = f(xg)

which is analogous to that of the regular representation.
It follows from this that Ind(g) permutes the subspaces Fs, and more precisely that Ind(g)
sends Fs to Fsg−1. In other words, in terms of the direct sum decomposition above, the action of
Ind(g) is a “block-permutation matrix”. Taking the trace (it helps visualizing this as a permutation
matrix), we see that it is the sum of the trace of the maps on Fs induced by Ind(g) where s runs
over those cosets s ∈H\G for which Hsg−1 = Hs, i.e., over those s for which sgs−1 ∈H.
Now for any s ∈G with sgs−1 ∈H, we compute the trace of the linear map

πs,g : Fs −→Fs

induced by Ind(g). To do this, we use the fact – already used in Proposition 2.3.11 – that Fs is
isomorphic to E, as a k-vector space. More precisely, there are reciprocal k-linear isomorphisms

E
α
−→Fs
β
−→E

such that
β(f) = f(s)

on the one hand, and α(v) is the element of Fs mapping hs to ϱ(h)v (and all x /∈Hs to 0.) The
fact that α and β are inverses of each other is left for the reader to prove (it is contained in the
proof of Proposition 2.3.11).
Thus the trace of Ind(g) is the sum, over those s with sgs−1 ∈H, of the trace of the linear map
on E given by β ◦πs,g ◦α. But – and this shouldn’t be much of a surprise – this map is simply
given by
ϱ(sgs−1) : E −→E,

with trace χϱ(sgs−1) (which is defined because sgs−1 ∈H, of course). The stated formula follows
by summing over the relevant s.
We check the claim: given v ∈E, and f = α(v), we have

(β ◦πs,g ◦α)(v) = Ind(g)f(s) = f(sg)

= f((sgs−1)s) = ϱ(sgs−1)f(s) = ϱ(sgs−1)v,

using the definitions of α and β.
□

The formula for an induced character may look strange or complicated at first. In particular, it
is probably not clear just by looking at the right-hand side that it is the character of a representation
of G! Nevertheless, we will see, here and especially in Chapter 4, that the formula is quite flexible
and much nicer than it may seem.

Exercise 2.7.47. Prove that if m ⩾n ⩾0, the characters of the two sides of the Clebsch-

The first part of Proposition 2.7.43 shows that the subset of Ck(G) whose elements are charac-
ters of finite-dimensional k-representations of G is stable under addition and multiplication. It is
therefore quite natural to consider the abelian group generated by all characters, as a subgroup of
the additive group of Ck(G). Indeed, the tensor product formula shows that this group is in fact a
ring.

88


Definition 2.7.48 (Generalized, or virtual, characters). Let G be a group and let k be a field
of characteristic zero. The character ring Rk(G) of generalized characters of G over k, is the ring
generated, as an abelian group, by the characters of finite-dimensional k-representations of G. The
elements of Rk(G) are also called virtual characters. The dimension of a virtual character χ is
defined to be χ(1) ∈Z ⊂k.

Note that Rk(G) is not a k-vector space: we do not allow linear combinations of characters
with coefficients which are not integers. Concretely, a virtual character χ ∈Rk(G) is a function

χ : G →k

of the form
χ = χϱ1 −χϱ2
for some finite-dimensional k-representations ϱ1 and ϱ2 of G; note that ϱ1 and ϱ2 are by no means
unique (e.g., one may replace ϱ1 by ϱ1 ⊕ϱ3, and ϱ2 by ϱ2 ⊕ϱ3, for an arbitrary finite-dimensional
representation ϱ3.)

Example 2.7.49 (Induced representations as an ideal in Rk(G)). Consider the ring Rk(G)
of a group G and a homomorphism ϕ : H →G with image Im(ϕ) of finite index in G. Now
consider the subgroup Iϕ of Rk(G) generated – as abelian group – by all characters of induced
representations IndG
H(ϱ), where ϱ is a finite-dimensional representation of H. Then the projection
formula (Proposition 2.3.18) shows that Iϕ ⊂Rk(G) is an ideal, i.e., χ1χ2 ∈Iϕ if χ1 in Iϕ and χ2 is
arbitrary.

We will present some applications of the character ring in Section 4.8.1. We will also say quite
a bit more about characters, for finite and compact groups, in Chapters 4 and 5.

2.8. Some Clifford theory

We have already observed that, in general, there are no obvious relations between irreducible
representations of a group G and those of a subgroup H. We consider here a special case of this
question, as an illustration of many aspects of the general formalism. This is the case when we
have a group G with an exact sequence

1 −→H −→G −→A −→1

and the quotient group A is abelian.
We can then find some precise links between irreducible
representations of G and those of H. This situation, although it seems rather special, does have
important applications, in particular in number theory. In these, it is usually the case that the
representation theory for H is considered to be simpler: this indicates why we attempt to understand
representations of G by restricting them to H.
The first result does not require that A be abelian.

Proposition 2.8.1. Let k be an algebraically closed field, let G be a group and H ◁G a normal
subgroup. Let ϱ be a finite-dimensional irreducible representation of G. Then either ResG
H(ϱ) is an
isotypic representation of H, or else there exists a proper subgroup H1 ⊃H of G and an irreducible
representation π of H1 such that ϱ ≃IndG
H1(π). In that second case, the character of ϱ is zero
outside of the subgroup H1.

Proof. First, by Exercise 2.3.4,20 the restriction of ϱ to H is semisimple. Let E be the space
on which ϱ acts, and let
E =
�

π
M(π)

20 If one does not wish to use this exercise, one might just add this as an assumption, and note that it holds in
the cases where all k-representations of G are semisimple, e.g., when G is finite.

89


be the canonical decomposition of E, as a representation of H, into a direct sum of isotypic com-
ponents, each of which is an isotypic representation attached to an irreducible representation π of
H. If there is only a single term in this direct sum, then by definition we are in the case where
ResG
H(ϱ) is isotypic. Thus we assume that this is not the case.
Because H is normal in G, the group G acts on the H-subrepresentations of E: if F ⊂E is
H-stable, then ϱ(g)F is also an H-subrepresentation since

ϱ(h)(ϱ(g)v) = ϱ(g)ϱ(g−1hg)v ∈ϱ(g)F.

This formula also indicates that in this action, any H-irreducible subrepresentation F isomorphic
to some given representation π is sent by ϱ(g) to a subrepresentation isomorphic to the represen-
tation
g �→π(g−1hg)

of H, which is also irreducible (the H-subrepresentations of F and ϱ(g)F correspond under ϱ(g) for
the same reasons.) In other words, G acts on the finite set of H-isotypic components M(π).
This action is transitive, because the direct sum of the isotypic components corresponding to
any non-empty G-orbit is a G-subrepresentation of E, hence equal to E since we assumed that ϱ is
irreducible.
Now fix some isotypic component π1 occurring in E, and let H1 denote the stabilizer of F =
M(π1) for this action of G. We note that H1 contains H, since M(π1) is an H-subrepresentation of
E, and is not equal to G, since otherwise E would be H-isotypic, which we assumed here is not the
case. Also the index of H1 in G is finite since dim E < +∞.
By definition of the stabilizer, the isotypic space F is an H1-subrepresentation of E. Similarly,
all other isotypic spaces are stable under the action of H1 (since they are of the form ϱ(g)F for
some g ∈G). We therefore find a decomposition

E =
�

π
M(π)

into H1-subrepresentations, where each summand is of the form ϱ(g)F for some g ∈G. The number
of these summands is |G|/|H1| = [G : H1] (since G acts transitively on the set of summands and H1
is the stabilizer of F). Thus Proposition 2.3.15 allows us to recognize that E ≃IndG
H1(F). Now we
conclude that F is irreducible as a representation of H1 because ϱ was assumed to be irreducible
(see Exercise 2.3.14).
The final statement concerning the character values was already mentioned in Example 2.7.44,
(3).
□

Now we consider some special properties of the case where the quotient A is abelian.

Proposition 2.8.2. Let k be an algebraically closed field, let G be a group and H ◁G a normal
subgroup such that A = G/H is abelian.
Let ϱ1 and ϱ2 be finite-dimensional irreducible k-representations of G acting on E1 and E2
respectively. Then
ResG
H(ϱ1) ≃ResG
H(ϱ2)

if and only if there exists a one-dimensional representation χ of A such that

ϱ2 ≃ϱ1 ⊗χ

where χ is viewed as a character of G via the quotient map G −→A.

Proof. One implication is clear: since a character χ of A is extended to G in such a way its
kernel contains H, the restriction to H of ϱ ⊗χ is equal to that of ϱ, for any representation ϱ.

90


The converse seems intuitive enough, but the argument, easy as it might look, is a rather good
illustration of the formalism of representation theory, and the reader might want to try to solve the
problem by herself first (see also the following Remark 2.8.3.)
We consider the space Homk(E1, E2) with its natural action (2.15) of G and the subspace
E = HomH(E1, E2) = Homk(E1, E2)H. The space E is non-zero, since the restrictions of ϱ1 and
ϱ2 to H are isomorphic. Since H is normal in G, E is a G-subrepresentation of Homk(E1, E2) (see
Lemma 2.1.12), and since H acts trivially, this means that E is a representation of the abelian
quotient A. Since E ̸= 0, this representation contains some irreducible subrepresentation, which
is one-dimensional (because A is abelian and k is algebraically closed, Corollary 2.7.17). Let χ be
such an irreducible representation of A in E. Now one can either deduce from the G-intertwiner

χ �→Homk(ϱ1, ϱ2) ≃qϱ1 ⊗ϱ2

that, by “twisting” by χ−1 = qχ, we have

1 �→(ϱ1 ⊗χ)q⊗ϱ2

and hence ϱ1 ⊗χ ≃ϱ2 by Schur’s Lemma (both are irreducible), or more concretely, one can take
a non-zero element Φ ∈E in the space of the subrepresentation isomorphic to χ. Spelling out what
this means, one finds that
ϱ2(g)Φ(ϱ1(g−1)v) = χ(g)Φ(v)

for all g ∈G and v ∈E1, which means that Φ ∈HomG(ϱ1, ϱ2 ⊗χ−1). Since it is non-zero, Schur’s
Lemma again proves that ϱ1 ≃ϱ2 ⊗χ−1.
□

Remark 2.8.3. We used the representation on Homk(ϱ1, ϱ2) to compare the two representations
ϱ1 and ϱ2. It is worth contrasting this argument with the following “cruder” version: since both
representations are isomorphic on H, they have in particular the same dimension, and composing
with an H-isomorphism, we are reduced to the case where ϱ1 and ϱ2 act on the same space E,
and satisfy ϱ1(h) = ϱ2(h) for all h ∈H. Now one can check directly that, for any fixed g ∈G,
the linear map α(g) = ϱ2(g)−1 ◦ϱ1(g) on E is an H-intertwiner. If we assume that ResG
H(ϱ1) is
irreducible, then Schur’s Lemma shows that α(g) = χ(g)Id for some scalar χ(g) ∈k×, and of course
it is then easy to check that χ is a character, and hence that ϱ1 = ϱ2 ⊗χ. The problem with this
down-to-earth reasoning is that it is rather messy to try to weaken the assumption of irreducibility
of the restriction to H! Philosophically, it is not representation-theoretic enough...

as representation of G, where HomH(ϱ1, ϱ2) is given the structure of a G-representation as in the

2.9. Conclusion

We have now built, in some sense, the basic foundations of representation theory. Interestingly,
there are other basic results, also valid in great generality, for which no proof is known using only
the methods of this chapter (which consist, essentially, of elementary abstract algebra). These
statements require the theory of algebraic groups. A very short introduction, with a discussion of
some of the results it leads to, can be found in the beginning of Chapter 7.
Before closing this chapter, we can use the vocabulary we have accumulated to ask: What are
the fundamental questions of representation theory? The following are maybe the two most obvious
ones:

• (Classification) For a group G, one may want to classify all its irreducible representations
(say over the complex numbers), or all representations of a certain type. This is possible in
a number of very important cases, and is often of tremendous importance for applications.
One should think here of a group G which is fairly well-understood from a group-theoretic
point of view; knowing its representations is a natural way to deepen the understanding
of the group.
• (Decomposition) Given a group G again, and a representation E of G, which arises nat-
urally in some application, one may ask to find explicitly the irreducible components of
E, either as summands if E is semisimple, or simply as composition factors. If the group
is sufficiently complicated, this decomposition might involve a “direct integral” (the sim-
plest example comes from the theory of Fourier integrals, see Section 7.3) and not merely
a direct sum.
In Chapters 4 and 5, we will see a number of examples of the first problem for specific cases
of finite groups, and for some compact topological groups. Indeed, this problem is rather well-
understood for some of these groups, including very important ones for applications. We will also
see a few cases of the second problem, one of them being responsible for some of the basic properties
of the hydrogen atom (see Section 6.4).
Of course, these few examples are only sample illustrations.
Among the areas of modern
mathematics where one finds many others, we will just mention that representation theory is
absolutely crucial to the so-called “Langlands Program” in number theory. We refer to [3] for a
general survey of many aspects of this theory.

92


CHAPTER 3

Variants

We present here some of the variants of the notions of representation theory that were discussed
in the previous chapter. They are all very important topics in their own right, and we will encounter
them intermittently in later chapters.

3.1. Representations of algebras

If, instead of a group G, we consider an algebra A over a field k, i.e., a (not necessarily commu-
tative) ring which has a compatible structure of k-vector space, the analogue of a representation of
A is an algebra homomorphism
A
ϱ
−→Endk(E).

This may be called a representation of A, but it is more usual to focus on E and to note that
such a map defines a structure of A-module on the vector space E by

a · v = ϱ(a)v

for a ∈A and v ∈E.
It is important for certain aspects of representation theory that the k-representations of a group
G can be understood in this language. Associated to G is the vector space k(G) freely generated
by G (which already appeared at the beginning of the previous chapter). Using temporarily the
notation [g] to indicate the g-th basis vector of k(G), this vector space has a structure of k-algebra
if one defines the product on k(G) by

[g] · [h] = [gh],

and extends it by linearity. Thus, given elements ℓ, m ∈k(G) which we express as linear combina-
tions

Definition 3.1.2 (Group algebra). Let k be a field and G a group. The algebra k(G) defined
above is called the group algebra of G over k.

The characteristic algebraic property of the group algebra is the following:

Proposition 3.1.3 (Representations as modules over the group algebra). Let G be a group and
k a field. If
G
ϱ
−→GL(E)
is a k-representation of E, then E has a structure of k(G)-module defined by extending ϱ by linearity,
i.e.,
��

g∈G
λg[g]
�

�

g∈G
λgϱ(g)v.

Conversely, any k(G)-module E inherits a representation of G by restricting the “multiplication
by a” maps to the basis vectors [g], i.e., by putting

ϱ(g) = (v �→[g] · v),

and furthermore, a k-linear map Φ : ϱ1 −→ϱ2 is a G-morphism if and only if Φ is k(G)-linear.

Sometimes, one denotes by ϱ(a) the map v �→a·v. Thus ϱ(a) becomes an element in Homk(E, E).

Here are some reasons why this approach can be very fruitful:

• It gives access to all the terminology and results of the theory of algebras; in particular,
for any ring A, the notions of sums, intersections, direct sums, etc, of A-modules, are
well-defined and well-known. For A = k(G), they correspond to the definitions already
given in the previous chapter for linear representations. Other constructions are however
more natural in the context of algebras. For instance, one may consider the ideals of k(G)
and their properties, which is not something so natural at the level of the representations
themselves.
• The k(G)-modules parametrize, in the almost tautological way we have described, the
k-representations of G.
It may happen that one wishes to concentrate attention on a
special class C of representations, characterized by some property. It happens sometimes
that these representations correspond in a natural way to all (or some of) the representa-
tions of another group GC . An example is to consider for C the class of one-dimensional
representations, which correspond to those of the abelianized group G/[G, G], as in Propo-
sition 2.3.3. This phenomenon is rather rare, but it may happen more often that there is
a natural k-algebra AC such that its modules correspond precisely (and “naturally”) to
the representations in C .
Partly for reasons of personal habit (or taste), we won’t exploit the group algebra systematically
in this book. This can be justified by the fact that it is not absolutely necessary at the level we
work. But we will say a bit more, e.g., in Section 4.3.6, and Exercise 4.3.31 describes a property of
representations with cyclic vectors which is much more natural from the point of view of the group
algebra.

94

3.2. Representations of Lie algebras

This section can be safely skipped in a first reading, since it will only be mentioned again in
Chapters 6 and 7. However, it should become clear then (especially from the analysis of represen-
tations of SL2(R) in Section 7.4) that the concepts we quickly discuss here are of great importance
and usefulness.
Besides associative algebras, as in the previous section, there exist other important and natural
algebraic structures which lead to corresponding representation theories. A particularly appealing
one is that of a “quiver”, for which an excellent introduction is found in [22, §5], but the most
important case, besides that of groups and algebras, is probably that of Lie algebras, because of
the intimate connection between Lie groups and Lie algebras.
We just present here only the simplest definitions, and one of the most basic and crucial results
in the representation theory of Lie algebras: the classification of the finite-dimensional irreducible
representations of the 3-dimensional simple complex Lie algebra sl2.

Definition 3.2.1 (Lie algebra). Let k be a field. A Lie algebra over k is a k-vector space L
given with a k-bilinear map

[·, ·]
�
L × L
−→
L
(x, y)
�→
[x, y]

96


often called the (Lie) bracket of L, which satisfies the following rules:

[x, x] = 0 for all x ∈L

[[x, y], z] + [[y, z], x] + [[z, x], y] = 0 for all x, y, z ∈L.

The second condition is called the Jacobi identity.1

Remark 3.2.2. If the characteristic of k is not equal to 2, the first condition can be replaced
by
[x, y] = −[y, x]
for all x, y in L (indeed, this implies the condition we stated if 2 is invertible, and conversely, using
the bilinarity, we see that [x + y, x + y] = 0 implies that [x, y] = −[y, x].

There are two major natural sources of Lie algebras. One is the tangent space at the identity of
a Lie group, with its Lie bracket (see Section 6.1), or the Lie algebra of smooth vector fields on an
arbitrary smooth manifold, which are real Lie algebras, and the other is any associative k-algebra
A, which has an associated Lie algebra structure obtained by defining

[x, y] = xy −yx

for (x, y) ∈A × A. Indeed, this clearly defines a k-bilinear operation on A which is antisymmetric,
and the Jacobi identity is obtained by a straightforward computation:

[[x, y], z] + [[y, z], x] = (xy −yx)z −z(xy −yx) + (yz −zy)x −x(yz −zy)
= −yxz −zxy + yzx + xzy

= y(zx −xz) −(zx −xz)y

= [y, [z, x]] = −[[z, x], y].

In particular, if E is a k-vector space, we obtain a Lie algebra structure, denoted gl(E), on the
k-algebra Endk(E).

Definition 3.2.3 (Homomorphisms, representations). Let k be a field.
(1) A morphism between Lie algebras L1 and L2 over k is a k-linear map ϕ : L1 −→L2 such
that ϕ([x, y]) = [ϕ(x), ϕ(y)] for all x, y ∈L1. A subspace M of a Lie algebra L is a subalgebra if the
restriction of the bracket to M gives it a Lie algebra structure, i.e., if [x, y] ∈M for all x, y in M.
(2) A k-representation of a k-Lie algebra L is a morphism

ϕ : L −→gl(E)

for some k-vector space E. One says that L “acts on E by ϕ”, and one writes x·v instead of ϕ(x)(v)
for x ∈L and v ∈E.
(3) A homomorphism ϕ1 −→ϕ2 of representations of L, with ϕ1 and ϕ2 acting on E1 and E2
respectively, is a k-linear map E1
Φ
−→E2 such that

Φ(x · v) = Φ(x) · v

for all x ∈L and v ∈E1.

Example 3.2.4. (The statements in these examples are simple exercises that the reader should
work out on her own.)
(1) Let E be a k-vector space. The subspace

sl(E) = {x ∈gl(E) = Endk(E) | Tr(x) = 0}

1 A mnemonic: a sum of three iterated brackets, where the bracket positions are fixed, and the arguments are
permuted cyclically.

97


is a subalgebra of gl(E). Similarly, if B is a bilinear form defined on E, the subspace

o(E, B) = {x ∈gl(E) | B(x · v, w) + B(v, x · w) = 0}

of gl(E) is a subalgebra of E. (To check this, it may be simpler to express B in the form B(v, w) =
tvMw for some matrix M.)

(2) Let A be an associative k-algebra. A derivation ∂of A is a k-linear map A
∂
−→A such that

∂(xy) = x∂(y) + ∂(x)y

for all x, y ∈A. The set Derk(A) of all derivations of A is a vector subspace of Endk(A), and a
simple computation (left as exercise) shows that Derk(A) is a subalgebra of gl(A). The name of
derivation is justified, of course, by the example where A is the algebra of smooth functions defined
on R, and ∂f = f′ is the derivative of a function f ∈A.
(3) Let L be a k-Lie algebra. The adjoint representation of L is the representation

ad : L −→gl(L)

of L on its underlying vector space L that is defined by

ad(x)(y) = [x, y]

for all x, y in L. It is indeed a representation, and in fact this property is equivalent with the Jacobi
identity. Furthermore, another easy computation using the Jacobi identity shows that ad(x) is a
derivation of Endk(E).
It is clear that the kernel of ad is the center

z(L) = {x ∈L | [x, y] = 0 for all y ∈L}

of L. For instance, one deduces easily that the adjoint representation of sl(E) is injective.

There is a formalism of representations of Lie algebras that closely parallels the formalism
for groups, and which is indeed directly related when specialized to Lie groups. For instance, an
irreducible representation is one of dimension ⩾1 where there is no non-zero subspace that is stable
under the action of the Lie algebra.

Exercise 3.2.5. Let k be an algebraically closed field and L a k-Lie algebra. We denote by
HomL(ϕ1, ϕ2) the vector space of homomorphisms of Lie-algebra representations between two such
representations ϕ1 and ϕ2.

for instance (3.4).]
(3) Given a representation ϕ of L acting on E, can you define a representation of L on Endk(E)
such that the space of invariants of this representation is equal to HomL(ϕ, ϕ)?

We will present only one gem from the vast field of Lie algebras: we will determine the finite-
dimensional irreducible representations of the complex Lie algebra sl(C2), or in other words, of the
space of complex matrices of size 2 with trace 0.

Sketch of proof. First, we denote L = sl(C2) for simplicity.
Then we observe that the

form a basis of L (as a vector space). A straightforward computation shows that they satisfy the

[h, e] = 2e,
[h, f] = −2f,
[e, f] = h.
We see from these in particular that both h and ad(h) are diagonalizable with integral eigen-
values (in the basis (e, h, f) of L, the matrix of ad(h) is diagonal with eigenvalues (2, 0, −2)). This
fact will generalize to all irreducible representations of L, and it is a crucial point.
Let ϕ : L −→gl(E) be a finite-dimensional irreducible representation of L. We attempt to
understand the decomposition of ϕ(h) in eigenspaces in order to determine the structure of E. One
first easy observation is that Tr(ϕ(h)) = Tr(ϕ([e, f])) = Tr([ϕ(e), ϕ(f)]) is equal to zero.
Let λ be an eigenvalue of ϕ(h), selected so that Re(λ) is as large as possible, and let v be a
non-zero λ-eigenvector of ϕ(h). (This exists because E is non-zero and finite-dimensional, and C is
algebraically closed.)
We first observe that ϕ(e)v = 0. Indeed, using the relations above and the fact that ϕ is a
representation, we have

ϕ(h)ϕ(e)v = ϕ(e)ϕ(h)v + ϕ([h, e])v = λϕ(e)v + 2ϕ(e)v = (λ + 2)ϕ(e)v.

Thus ϕ(e)v is in the (λ + 2)-eigenspace of ϕ(v); but the maximality condition imposed on λ
shows that this eigenspace must be 0, and hence ϕ(e)v = 0.
Next, consider the vectors vi = ϕ(f)iv for i ⩾0 (the power refers to repeated compositions in
Endk(E)). By induction, using the relation [h, f] = −2f, one sees that vi satisfies

ϕ(h)vi = (λ −2i)vi.

Since dim E is finite, all these vi must be zero from some point on – otherwise we would get
infinitely many distinct eigenvectors of ϕ(h). Hence there exists n ⩾0 be such that vn ̸= 0 but
vi = 0 for all i ⩾n + 1.
We denote w = vn and µ = λ −2n, its eigenvalue. Now, we go back “upwards”, defining
wi = ϕ(e)vi+1 for 0 ⩽i < n. The point is that

wi = (i + 1)(λ −i)vi

for all i. This is proved by induction on i, using the relation [e, f] = h: for instance, for i = 0, we
have
w0 = ϕ(e)ϕ(f)v = ϕ(f)ϕ(e)v + ϕ(h)v = λv = λv0
(since ϕ(e)v = 0) and for i = 1 we get

w1 = ϕ(e)v2 = ϕ(e)ϕ(f)v1
= ϕ(f)ϕ(e)v1 + ϕ(h)v1 = ϕ(f)w0 + (λ −2)v1,

99


which (using w0 = λv0 and ϕ(f)v0 = v1) gives

w1 = λv1 + (λ −2)v1 = 2(λ −1)v1 ;

this is the case i = 1, and the induction is completely similar.
A direct consequence of this formula is that the span of (v0, . . . , vn) is a non-zero subrepresen-
tation of E. Thus, by irreducibility, we have

To conclude the proof, we therefore only need to prove the existence and uniqueness of a
representation of dimension n + 1.
We leave these as exercises, with some hints: for uniqueness, we can start from a non-zero
vector v with ϕ(h)v = nv and ϕ(e)v = 0, and the only subtlety is to check that w = ϕ(f)nv is
non-zero, to deduce that the vectors

(3.3)

The existence can then also be checked by hand: for any fixed basis (v0, . . . , vn) of an (n + 1)-
dimensional vector space E, we define linear maps ˜h, ˜e and ˜f on E by

˜hvi = (n −2i)vi,
˜fvi = vi+1,

and by following the rules for ˜e found in expressing ϕ(e)vi in terms of the basis (3.3) during the
uniqueness step. We may then check by brute force that these linear maps satisfy the commutation
relations
[˜h, ˜e] = 2˜e,
[˜h, ˜f] = −2 ˜f,
[˜e, ˜f] = ˜h,

and this implies that the map ϕ given by

ϕ(αe + βf + γh) = α˜e + β ˜f + γ˜h,

for (α, β, γ) ∈C3, is a representation of L. See also, for instance, [39, §4.8] for a slightly different
approach.

3.3. Topological groups

In many applications, it is particularly important to restrict the representations to respect some
additional structure. Among these, topological conditions are the most common.
The corresponding structure is that of a topological group, i.e., a group G equipped with a
topology such that the product map
G × G →G

and the inverse map
G →G

are both continuous (with G×G being given the product topology). There are many examples: for
instance, any finite group can be seen as a topological group with the discrete topology; the additive
group R or the multiplicative group R×, with the usual euclidean topology, are also topological
groups; similarly, for any n ⩾1, GLn(C) is a group with the topology coming from its inclusion
in Mn(C) ≃Cn2; and moreover, any subgroup H of a topological group G in G inherits from
G a topology and is then a topological group. This includes, for instance, groups like Z ⊂R,
SLn(R) ⊂GLn(R) or SLn(Z) ⊂SLn(R).
When dealing with a topological group, one typically wishes to restrict the representations
which are considered to include some continuity property. This usually means taking the base field
to be either k = C or R, and ensuring that the vector space carries a suitable topology.2 We
will restrict our attention to Banach spaces, i.e., k-vector spaces E with a norm ∥· ∥on E such
that E is complete for this norm. Of special interest, in fact, will be Hilbert spaces, when the
norm derives from an inner product ⟨·, ·⟩. As a special case, it is important to recall that if E is
a finite-dimensional real or complex vector space, it carries a unique topology of Banach space,
which can be defined using an inner product if desired. (Though, as is well-known, there are many
equivalent norms which can be used to define this topology.)
Given a topological group G and a Banach space E, the first restriction concerning representa-
tions ϱ of G on E is that the operators ϱ(g), g ∈G, should be continuous. Precisely, we denote by
L(E) the space of continuous linear maps

T : E −→E,

(which are also called bounded linear maps) and by BGL(E) the group of those T ∈L(E) that
are invertible, i.e., bijective with a continuous inverse. In fact, this last continuity condition is
automatic when E is a Banach space, by the Banach isomorphism theorem (see, e.g., [53, Th.
III.11].) Of course, if dim(E) < +∞, we have BGL(E) = GL(E).
If L(E) is given any topology, the group BGL(E) inherits, as a subset of L(E) a topology from
the latter. It is then natural to think of considering representations

ϱ : G −→BGL(E)

that are continuous. However, as readers familiar with functional analysis will already know, quite
a few different topologies are commonly encountered on L(E).
The most natural3 is the norm
topology, associated to the norm

(3.5)
∥T∥L(E)| = sup
v∈E
v̸=0

∥Tv∥

∥v∥= sup
v∈E
∥v∥=1

∥T(v)∥

2 Although other fields with a topology do arise. For instance, in number theory and algebraic geometry, one
often encounters p-adic fields.
3 It is natural, for instance, because L(E) becomes a Banach space for this norm.

102


defined for T ∈L(E).
In other words, it is the topology of uniform convergence on bounded
subsets of E; the two expressions coincide because of linearity. However, it turns out that asking
for homomorphisms to BGL(E) which are continuous for this topology does not lead to a good
theory: there are “too few” representations in that case, as we will illustrate below. The “correct”
definition is the following:

Definition 3.3.1 (Continuous representation). Let G be a topological group and k = R or C.
Let E be a k-Banach space.
(1) A continuous representation, often simply called a representation, of G on E is a homomor-
phism
ϱ : G −→BGL(E)
such that the corresponding action map�
G × E −→E
(g, v) �→ϱ(g)v

is continuous, where G × E has the product topology.
(2) If ϱ1 and ϱ2 are continuous representations of G, acting on E1 and E2 respectively, a
homomorphism
ϱ1
Φ
−→ϱ2
is a continuous linear map E1 −→E2 such that

Φ(ϱ1(g)v) = ϱ2(g)Φ(v)

for all g ∈G and v ∈E1.

If dim(E) < +∞, note that this is indeed equivalent to asking that ϱ be continuous, where
BGL(E) has its usual (euclidean) topology.

Example 3.3.2 (Too many representations). Consider G = R and k = C. If we consider simply
one-dimensional representations
R →C×,
with no continuity assumptions at all, there are “too many” for most purposes. Indeed, as an
abelian group, R has infinite rank, and it is a relatively direct application of Zorn’s Lemma that
any homomorphism χ : H −→C×, where H ⊂R is any subgroup, can be extended to a one-
dimensional representation of R. Since R is torsion free, it follows that for any finite subset J ⊂R
which generate a free abelian group of rank |J|, and any complex numbers zj for j ∈J, we can find
a one-dimensional representation χ such that χ(j) = zj for j ∈J.
But as soon as we impose some regularity on the homomorphisms R →C×, we obtain a much
better understanding:

Proposition 3.3.3 (Continuous characters of R). Let χ : R →C× be a continuous group
homomorphism. Then there exists a unique s ∈C such that

χ(x) = esx

for all x ∈R.

In fact, one can show that it is enough to ask that the homomorphism be measurable. The
intuitive meaning of this is that, if one can “write down” a homomorphism R →C× in any concrete
way, or using standard limiting processes, it will automatically be continuous, and hence it will be
one of the ones above.
The proof we give uses differential calculus, but one can give completely elementary arguments
(as in [2, Example A.2.5]).

103

for some parameter α ∈C. In our case, putting x = 0 leads to 1 = χ(0) = α, and hence we get the
result.

Example 3.3.4 (Too few representations). Let G be the compact group R/Z. We now show
that, if one insisted on considering as representations only homomorphisms

ϱ : R/Z −→BGL(E)

which are continuous with respect to the norm topology on BGL(E), there would be “too few”
(similar examples hold for many other groups). In particular, there would be no good analogue of
the regular representation. Indeed, if f is a complex-valued function on R/Z and t ∈R/Z, it is
natural to try to define the latter with

ϱ(t)f(x) = f(x + t).

To have a Banach space of functions, we must impose some regularity condition. Although the
most natural spaces turn out to be the Lp spaces, with respect to Lebesgue measure, we start with
E = C(R/Z), the space of continuous functions f : R/Z →C. If we use the supremum norm

∥f∥= sup
t∈R/Z
|f(t)|,

this is a Banach space. Moreover, the definition above clearly maps a function f ∈E to ϱ(t)f ∈E;
indeed, we have
∥ϱ(t)f∥= ∥f∥

104


(since the graph of ϱ(t)f, seen as a periodic function on R, is just obtained from that of f by
translating it to the left by t units), and this further says that ϱ(t) is a continuous linear map on
E. Hence, ϱ certainly defines a homomorphism

ϱ : R/Z −→BGL(E).

But now we claim that ϱ is not continuous for the topology on BGL(E) coming from the
norm (3.5). This is quite easy to see: in fact, for any t ̸= 0 with 0 < t < 1/2, we have

∥ϱ(t) −IdE∥L(E) = ∥ϱ(t) −ϱ(0)∥L(E) ⩾1

which shows that ϱ is very far from being continuous at 0. To see this, we take as “test” function
any ft ∈E which is zero outside [0, t], non-negative, always ⩽1, and equal to 1 at t/2, for instance
(where we view R/Z as obtained from [0, 1] by identifying the end points).

Then ∥ft∥= 1, and therefore

∥ϱ(t) −Id∥L(E) ⩾∥ϱ(t)ft −ft∥=
sup
x∈R/Z
|ft(t + x) −ft(x)| = 1

since ϱ(t)ft is supported on the image modulo Z of the interval [1−t, t] which is disjoint from [0, t],
apart from the common endpoint t.
However, the point of this is that we had to use a different test function for each t: for a fixed
f, the problem disappears, since by uniform continuity of f ∈E, we have

lim
t→0 ∥ϱ(t)f −f∥= 0

for any fixed f ∈E.

Exercise 3.3.5. Show that ϱ defined above on E = C(R/Z) is a continuous representation in
the sense of Definition 3.3.1.

The general formalism of representation theory can, to a large extent, be transferred or adapted
to the setting of representations of topological groups. In particular, for finite-dimensional represen-
tations, since all operations considered are “obviously continuous”, every construction goes through.
This applies to direct sums, tensor products, the contragredient, symmetric and exterior powers,
subrepresentations and quotients, etc.
Some care is required when considering infinite-dimensional representations. For instance, the
definition of subrepresentations and quotient representations, as well as that of irreducible repre-
sentation, should be adjusted to take the topology into account:

Definition 3.3.6 (Subrepresentations and irreducibility). Let G be a topological group, and
let
ϱ : G −→BGL(E)

be a representation of G on a Banach space E.

105


(1) A representation π of G is a subrepresentation of ϱ if π acts on a closed subspace F ⊂E
which is invariant under ϱ, and π(g) is the restriction of ϱ(g) to F. Given such a subrepresentation
π, the quotient ϱ/π is the induced representation on the quotient Banach space E/F with the norm

∥v∥E/F = min{∥w∥E | w (mod F) = v}.

(2) The representation ϱ is irreducible (sometimes called topologically irreducible ) if E is non-
zero and E has no non-zero proper subrepresentation, i.e., there is no non-zero proper closed
subspace F of E which is stable under all ϱ(g), g ∈G.

If dim(E) < +∞, since any subspace of F is closed, there is no difference between these defi-
nitions and the previous one. But in general the distinction between closed subspaces and general
subspaces is necessary to obtain a good formalism. There exist indeed infinite-dimensional rep-
resentations which are topologically irreducible but have many non-zero proper stable subspaces
(these are necessarily dense in the space E, but they are not closed.)
The second example we give of adapting the formalism is that of the contragredient represen-
tation. Given a Banach space E, the dual Banach space is the vector space E′ of continuous linear
maps E −→C with the norm

∥λ∥E′ = sup
∥v∥=1
|λ(v)| = sup
v̸=0

|λ(v)|

∥v∥.

Given a representation
ϱ : G −→BGL(E)
of a topological group G on E, the topological contragredient qϱ acts on E′ by the usual formula

⟨g · λ, v⟩= ⟨λ, ϱ(g−1)v⟩.

If E is finite-dimensional, this is obviously continuous, but in general this is a rather delicate
issue. One can show (see [10, p. 191, Exercise 3, (c)]) that the contragredient representation is
continuous if the Banach space E is reflexive (in particular, if E is a Hilbert space), but that it is
not in general, even if the representation is bounded (or even isometric; see [10, p. 191, Exercise
3, (d)] for a counterexample).
Although it is an abuse of notation, we will sometimes omit the adjective “topological” when
considering the contragredient of continuous representations. Unless otherwise specificed, whenever
we consider continuous representations of topological groups, the “contragredient” will mean the
topological contragredient.

3.4. Unitary representations

When the representation space (for a topological group) is a Hilbert space H, with an inner
product4 ⟨·, ·⟩, it is natural to consider particularly closely the unitary representations, which are
those for which the operators ϱ(g) are unitary, i.e., preserve the inner product:

⟨ϱ(g)v, ϱ(g)w⟩= ⟨v, w⟩

for all g ∈G, v and w ∈H.
We present here the most basic facts about such representations. They will be considered in
more detail first for finite groups (Chapter 4), and then for compact and locally compact groups
(Chapters 5 and 6 and Sections 7.2, 7.3, 7.4). For additional information on the general theory
of unitary representations, especially with respect to infinite-dimensional cases, we recommend the
Appendices to [2] as well as [48].

4 Recall from the introduction that our inner products are linear in the first variable, and conjugate-linear in the
second: ⟨v, λw⟩= ¯λ⟨v, w⟩for λ ∈C.

106


Definition 3.4.1 (Unitary representations, unitarizable representations). Let G be a topolog-
ical group.
(1) A unitary representation of G is a continuous representation of G on a Hilbert space H
where ϱ(g) ∈U(H) for all g ∈G, where U(H) is the group of unitary operators of G. A morphism
ϱ1 →ϱ2 of unitary representations, acting on H1 and H2 respectively, is a morphism of continuous
representations ϱ1
Φ
−→ϱ2 (one does not require that Φ preserve the inner product.)
(2) An arbitrary representation
G −→GL(E)
of G on a complex vector space E is unitarizable if there exists an inner product ⟨·, ·⟩on E, defining
a structure of Hilbert space, such that the values of ϱ are unitary for this inner product, and the
resulting map G −→U(E) is a unitary representation.

Remark 3.4.2. (1) If E is a finite-dimensional complex vector space and G carries the discrete
topology (e.g., if G is finite) it is enough to construct an inner product on the vector space E such
that ϱ takes value in U(E) in order to check that a representation is unitarizable (the continuity is
automatic).
(2) If ϱ1 : G −→U(H1) and ϱ2 : G −→U(H2) are unitary representations, and Φ : H1 −→H2
is a G-homomorphism, the adjoint Φ∗: H2 −→H1 of Φ is also an intertwiner. Indeed, given g ∈G,
and v ∈H2, we have (with obvious notation)

⟨ϱ1(g)(Φ∗(v)), w⟩1 = ⟨Φ∗(v), ϱ1(g−1)w⟩1

= ⟨v, Φ(ϱ1(g−1)w)⟩2

= ⟨v, ϱ2(g−1)Φ(w)⟩2
= ⟨ϱ2(g)v, Φ(w)⟩2 = ⟨Φ∗(ϱ2(g)v), w⟩1,

for all w ∈H1, which implies that Φ∗(ϱ2(g)v) = ϱ1(g)(Φ∗(v)), and hence that Φ∗intertwines ϱ2
and ϱ1.

The continuity requirement for unitary representations can be rephrased in a way which is
easier to check:

Proposition 3.4.3 (Strong continuity criterion for unitary representations). Let ϱ : G −→
U(H) be a homomorphism of a topological group G to a unitary group of some Hilbert space. Then ϱ
is a unitary representation, i.e., the action map is continuous, if and only if ϱ is strongly continuous:
for any fixed v ∈H, the map
�
G −→H
g �→ϱ(g)v

is continuous on G. Equivalently, this holds when these maps are continuous at g = 1.

Proof. The joint continuity of the two-variable action map implies that of the maps above,
which are one-variable specializations. For the converse, we use the unitarity and a splitting of
epsilons.
Let (g, v) be given in G × H. For any (h, w) ∈G × H, we have

∥ϱ(g)v −ϱ(h)w∥= ∥ϱ(h)(ϱ(h−1g)v −w)∥= ∥ϱ(h−1g)v −w∥

by unitarity. Then, by the triangle inequality, we get

∥ϱ(g)v −ϱ(h)w∥⩽∥ϱ(h−1g)v −v∥+ ∥v −w∥.

Under the assumption of the continuity of x �→ϱ(x)v when x →1, this shows that when w is
close to v and h close to g, the difference becomes arbitrarily small, which is the continuity of the

107


action map at (g, v). To be precise: given ε > 0, we can first find an open neighborhood Uv of v
in H such that ∥v −w∥< ε for w ∈Uv, and we can use the continuity assumption to find an open
neighborhood U1 of 1 ∈G such that ∥ϱ(x)v −v∥< ε for x ∈U1. Then, when (h, w) ∈gU−1
1
× Uv,
we have
∥ϱ(g)v −ϱ(h)w∥< 2ε.
□

Remark 3.4.4 (The strong topology). The name “strong continuity” refers to the fact that the
condition above is equivalent with the assertion that the homomorphism

ϱ : G −→U(H)

is continuous, with respect to the topology induced on U(H) by the so-called strong operator topology
on L(H). The latter is defined as weakest topology such that all linear maps

(for v ∈H) are continuous. This means that a basis of neighborhoods of T0 ∈L(H) for this topology
is given by the finite intersections

for some unit vectors vi ∈H and some fixed ε > 0 (see, e.g., [53, p. 183]).

Example 3.4.5 (The regular representation on L2(R)). Here is a first example of infinite-
dimensional unitary representation.
We consider the additive group R of real numbers, with
the usual topology, and the space H = L2(R) of square-integrable functions on R, defined us-
ing Lebesgue measure. Defining
ϱ(t)f(x) = f(x + t),
we claim that we obtain a unitary representation. This is formally obvious, but as we have seen in
Example 3.3.4, the continuity requirement needs some care. We will see this in a greater context
in Proposition 5.2.6 in Chapter 5, but we sketch the argument here.
First, one must check that the definition of ϱ(t)f makes sense (since elements of L2(R) are
not really functions): this is indeed so, because if we change a measurable function f on a set of
measure zero, we only change x �→f(x + t) on a translate of this set, which still has Lebesgue
measure zero.
Then the unitarity of the action is clear: we have
�

|ϱ(t)f(x)|2dx =

|f(x + t)|2dx =
�

and only continuity remains to be checked. This is done in two steps using Proposition 3.4.3 (the
reader can fill the outline, or look at Proposition 5.2.6). Fix f ∈L2(R), and assume first that it
is continuous and compactly supported. Then the continuity of t �→ϱ(t)f amounts to the limit

for all t ∈R, which follows from the dominated convergence theorem. Next, one uses the fact that
continuous functions with compact support form a dense subspace of L2(R) (for the L2-norm) in
order to extend this continuity statement to all f ∈L2(R).
